Source slide page 3 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials The prefix 'Nano' is derived from the Greek word for dwarf 1nm = 10-9m 1nm is approximately the length of 10 H or 5 Si atoms aligned in a line Dimensions of nanomaterials lie in between bulk material and atoms/molecules upto μm bulk properties are observed. For smaller sizes quantum effects are seen as the dimensions are closer to atoms or molecules 3 EV0000200 · EV0000201 · EV0000202
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Properties of nanomaterials are different from bulk materials Au-bulk is yellowish in colour, and Au- nanoparticles are red in colour Al which is stable becomes combustible in the nanoparticle range and is used as solid fuel in rocket propulsion Melting point of materials is lower in nano-range EV0000203 · EV0000204 · EV0000205
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Nanomaterials: Materials with at least one external dimension between 1 and 100 nm Nanoscience is the study of phenomena and synthesis, characteristisation , exploration and exploitation of nanostructured materials Nanotechnologies are the design, characterisation, production and application of structures, devices and systems by controlling shape and size at nanometer scale EV0000206 · EV0000207 · EV0000208
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Classification of Nanomaterials Zero dimensional nanomaterials (0D) : Materials having all the dimensions within the nanorange where no dimension is allowed to be outside of nanorange. Like quantum dots , nanoclustures eg. fullerene. One dimensional nanomaterials(1D): Materials having one of their dimensions out of the nanorange eg. nanotubes , nanowires Two dimensional nanomaterials (2D) : Materials having two of their dimensions out of the nanorange eg. Nanofilms, nanocoatings Three dimensional nanomaterials (3D): Materials having all the three dimensions out of the nanorange eg. Dispersions of nanoparticles, bundles of nanowires EV0000209 · EV0000210 · EV0000211
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Nanomaterials possess specific properties which maybe significantly different from bulk properties Reasons Large fraction of surface atoms / unit volume: Properties are dictated by surface atoms rather than bulk atoms Eg Pt sheet versus Pt powder Source:https://nanosense.sri.com/activities/sizematters/introduction/SM_Lesson1Student.pdf Large surface energy : Surface atoms are held with less force as compared to bulk. Surface activity high .Eg Al EV0000212 · EV0000213 · EV0000214
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Spatial confinement- (quantum confinement) [Image text, OCR] Quantum Confinement 3-D All carriers actasfree carriers inallthreedirections 2-DorQuantumWells 3DBulk Thecarriersactasfreecarriers inaplane First observed in semiconductorsystems 2DQuantum Well 1-DorQuantumWires Thecarriersarefreetomove downthedirectionofthewire O-DorQuantumDots IDQuantumWire Systemsinwhichcarriersare confined in all directions(no OD Quantum Dot free carriers) Source:https://slideplayer.com/slide/5849551/ EV0000215 · EV0000216 · EV0000217

Source slide page 10 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Properties of Nanomaterials Properties of any bulk material are independent of its size whereas properties of nanomaterials change with size Properties of a bulk material can be altered only by altering their structure and composition EV0000221 · EV0000222 · EV0000223
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Properties of Nanomaterials Properties of any bulk material are independent of its size whereas properties of nanomaterials change with size Properties of a bulk material can be altered only by altering their structure and composition EV0000224 · EV0000225 · EV0000226
  12. Source slide page 12 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Surface Area dependant properties Many physical and chemical properties of a material depend on its surface properties Surface area is enormously increased on moving from bulk to nano scale. Nanomaterials have a significant proportion of atoms existing at the surface Properties like catalytic activity, gas adsorption depend on surface area e.g. Bulk gold is catalytically inactive but gold nanoparticles are catalytically very active for selective redox reactions Surface energy is also high so chemical reactivity is high e.g. Aluminium becomes combustible [Image text, OCR] Surfacearea=6m² Surfacearea=12m² Surfacearea=24m² 1m 0.5m 0.25m 1m 0.5m 0.25m Source:https://ninithi.wordpress.com/nanoscale-why-size-matter/ EV0000227 · EV0000228 · EV0000229
  13. Source slide page 13 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Optical Properties - Increase in Energy gap Increases in energy gap between the valence band and conduction band As size of nanoparticles decreases the energy gap increases and the wavelength of light absorbed moves towards smaller values . This is referred to as ‘blue shift’ eg. colloidal suspensions of gold nanoparticles : Gold spheres of > 50 nm absorb at λ = 575 nm Gold spheres of 10- 20 nm absorb at λ = 524 nm Gold spheres of 2-5 nm absorb at λ = 517 nm Source::https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colloidal_gold EV0000230 · EV0000231 · EV0000232
  14. Source slide page 14 (extracted text): THANK YOU Department of Science and Humanities EV0000233 · EV0000234 · EV0000235

Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Due to surface scattering Electrical conductivity decreases due to surface scattering Electrons have a mean free path If dimensions of the nanomaterial is smaller than the mean free path of the electron, elastic or inelastic surface scattering can happen Elastic scattering will not affect conductivity but when inelastic scattering happens, the scattered electron loses its velocity and electrical conductivity decreases EV0000248 · EV0000249 · EV0000250

In the presence of magnetic field ,they get magnetised with high susceptibilities.   11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Nanomaterials Structure [Image text, OCR] GRAPHENE 0.142 nm LIGHT! RIGID 100x PERFECT STRONGER THERMAL THAN CONDVLTOR STEEL ALLOTRDPE OF CARBON A CRYSTALLINE Graphene is a crystalline allotrope of carbon with 2-dimensional properties. Its carbon atoms are densely packed in a regular atomic-scale chicken wire (hexagonal) pattern. Each atom has four bonds, one σ bond with each of its three neighbors and one π-bond that is oriented out of plane. The atoms are about 1.42 Å apart Graphene can self-repair holes in its sheets, when exposed to molecules containing carbon, such as hydrocarbons. Bombarded with pure carbon atoms, the atoms perfectly align into hexagons, completely filling the holes EV0000266 · EV0000267 · EV0000268

Step 6: Thermal Annealing (High-Temperature Reduction) : at 1000–2000°C or using strong reducing agent like ascorbic acid, This is the most effective method to convert rGO closer to graphene. Step 1: Mixing Graphite + NaNO₃ are added to cold concentrated H₂SO₄. Stirring is done in an ice bath (temperature < 20°C). [Image text, OCR] Graphite Reduced Grapheneoxide Grapheneoxide HO HO HO HO HO OH HO Oxidation Reduction Step 5: Ultrasonication in water → exfoliates sheets into Graphene Oxide (GO). Step 2: Oxidation 1. KMnO₄ is added slowly—temperature must be controlled (risk of explosion). 2. The mixture thickens and turns dark green/purple. 3. Reaction proceeds at 35–40°C for about 1–2 hours. Step 3: Exfoliation & Termination 1. Water is added slowly → the solution heats up. 2. The color changes to brown, indicating graphite oxide formation. 3. H₂O₂ is added → turns the mixture bright yellow. Mn(VII) → Mn(II) Step 4: Washing & Drying The product is washed with: Distilled water Dilute HCl Ethanol The solid is filtered and dried → Graphite Oxide EV0000275 · EV0000276 · EV0000277

Source slide page 16 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Glass Transition temperature All amorphous polymers, when cooled below a certain temperature, become stiff, hard, brittle and glassy but above this temperature they are soft, flexible and rubbery This transition temperature of a polymer from glassy state to rubbery state is called its glass transition temperature and is denoted by Tg The rubbery state on heating melts and starts flowing. This temperature is the melting point, Tm [Image text, OCR] Glassy State Tg Transition Glass (ew) iRubbery Rubbery Flow State Wisdous OW Temperature (k) Source:https://omnexus.specialchem.com/polymer-properties/properties/glass-transition-temperature EV0000344 · EV0000345 · EV0000346

Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Structure: Polymers made up of linear chains without bulky pendant groups are more crystalline e.g., Polyethylene(PE) is more crystalline then polyvinylacetate Polymers containing polar groups are more crystalline A close packing between polymeric chains because of strong secondary forces like dipole–dipole interactions or hydrogen bonding imparts a high degree of crystallinity e.g., PVC is more crystalline than PE EV0000365 · EV0000366 · EV0000367

Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Tensile strength: The amount of stress a polymer can take before undergoing permanent deformation Tensile strength depends on : Molecular mass - low molecular mass polymers are soft and gummy, high molecular mass polymers are tough and heat resistant By increasing molecular mass the tensile strength increases to a certain extent and then becomes constant Melt viscosity initially shows gradual increase then increases steeply with high molecular weight [Image text, OCR] Commercial polymers Tensile and mpact strength Viscosity 10,000 10,000,000 Molecularweight, degree of polymerization Source:https://polymerinnovationblog.com/polymer-composites-part-2-introduction-polymer-resins/ EV0000371 · EV0000372 · EV0000373

Source slide page 9 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Elasticity : The property of a polymer by which it deforms on applying force and regains original shape on release of force is called elasticity Elastomers have long coiled and entangled chains which straighten and orient themselves on stretching. When stress is removed they go back to coiled form For a polymer to show elasticity the individual chains should not break on prolonged stretching [Image text, OCR] ELASTOMERS Elastomer is defined as a long chain polymer which under stress undergoes elongation by several times and regains its original shape when the stress is fully released Stretched Returnedto randomization Source:https://slideplayer.com/slide/8755303/ EV0000377 · EV0000378 · EV0000379
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Presence of polar groups in the polymer chain results in strong secondary force of attraction between the chains making them stiff and inflexible Non polar groups have weak van der Waal’s attraction between them and hence they are more elastic Presence of bulky, aromatic and cyclic groups in the polymer decreases the elasticity of the polymer EV0000380 · EV0000381 · EV0000382
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers In elastic materials such as natural rubber, elasticity can be improved by introducing cross linking at suitable molecular positions like in vulcanisation In non-elastic materials, elasticity is achieved by using plasticisers , for example (Ph3PO4), which enter between the polymer chains and make a hard polymer more flexible Source:https://revision4gcses.wordpress.com/science/chemistry-2/polymers/ Source:https://www.diva-portal.org/smash/get/diva2:1312811/FULLTEXT01.pdf EV0000383 · EV0000384 · EV0000385
  12. Source slide page 12 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Chemical resistance: Resistance to swell, dissolve and get degraded in the presence of a solvent or chemical It depends on the chemical nature of monomeric units and their molecular arrangement Presence of polar and non-polar groups: Like dissolves like - A polymer is more soluble in a solvent of similar chemical structure Polymers having polar groups (–OH or –COOH groups) are usually attacked or dissolved by polar liquids such as water or alcohols. (e.g. PVA dissolves in water) Polymers with non-polar groups such as –CH3 and –C6H5 are not easily attacked by polar solvents but they easily swell and sometimes dissolve in non-polar solvents such as petrol, benzene and carbon tetrachloride EV0000386 · EV0000387 · EV0000388
  13. Source slide page 13 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Residual unsaturation leads to oxidative degradation: polymers with residual double bonds get attacked and degraded in air and UV light e.g.,natural rubber Source:https://www.embibe.com/study/natural-rubber-vulcanization-of-natural-rubber-concept?entity_code=KTBP50 Packing: Dense packing does not allow the solvent to penetrate between the layers hence resistance increases, for example Teflon Molecular mass: For a given polymer, the swelling character decreases with the increase in the molecular mass Degree of cross linking: Greater the degree of cross linking lesser is the solubility Source:https://pslc.ws/macrog/ptfe.htm EV0000389 · EV0000390 · EV0000391
  14. Source slide page 14 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Plastic deformation : Property of a polymer by which on application of heat it becomes soft and flexible and on cooling it becomes hard and brittle is called plasticity or plastic deformation Based on their response to heat two kinds of polymers are: Thermoplastic - A thermoplastic polymer is one which softens on heating and becomes hard on cooling. It can be remoulded into new shapes, e.g., Polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene Thermosetting - A thermosetting polymer becomes hard on heating. It cannot be remoulded, its structure instead undergoes a total degradation on heating, e.g.,Bakelite, Aniline aldehyde resin, urea formaldehyde resin EV0000392 · EV0000393 · EV0000394
  15. Source slide page 15 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers The reason for the difference in the thermal behaviour is: Thermoplastics have relatively weak forces of attraction like van der Waals forces, dipole-dipole attraction or H-bonding between the chains, which are overcome when the material is heated Thermosetting plastics have the cross-linking between the chains is by strong covalent bonds cannot be broken easily. On strong heating will result in charring and degradation [Image text, OCR] hermoplastic temoseting degracaton Source:http://chemistry2.csudh.edu/rpendarvis/Polymer.html EV0000395 · EV0000396 · EV0000397

Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Working: A voltage is applied across the anode and cathode of the OLED Electrons flows from cathode to anode through the organic layers Electrons are transported to the emissive layer from cathode Holes are transported to the conductive layer from anode Electrons combine with the holes thereby emitting light Recombination happens in the emissive layer Colour of light depends on the organic molecules used and intensity depends on current density [Image text, OCR] OLED Creating Light 2005HowSturtWorks ATL CATHODE CATHODE ANODE Conductive Emissive Layer Layer Electrical current flows from the cathode to the anode through the organic layers,giving electrons to the emissive layer andremoving electrons from the conductive layer. Electron Removing electrons from the conductive layer leaves holes that need to be filled with the electrons in the emissive layer. The holes jump to the emissive layerand recombine with the electrons.As the electrons drop into the holes,they release their extra energy as light.   10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Functional materials - Polymers Why Multi-Layered Structure? Inorganic semiconductors have a higher conductivity than the organic materials, so a layered structure is not needed. Organic materials have a lower conductivity than the inorganic counterparts HOMOs and LUMOs of the materials in the layered structure act as small steps and guide the charge carriers to the emissive layer. The layered structure helps maintain a smoother carrier flow. Anastas and John C. Warner EV0000626 · EV0000627 · EV0000628

Source slide page 4 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Green Chemistry focuses on : Reduction, recycling and/or eliminating toxic substances Finding creative, alternative routes to minimize impact on the environment More eco-friendly green alternative to conventional chemistry practices Provides sustainable development, sustainable business and sustainable living practices EV0000629 · EV0000630 · EV0000631

Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Twelve Principles of Green Chemistry Prevent Waste | Use of renewable feedstocks Atom Economy | Reduce Derivatives Safer Synthesis | Catalysis Safe Products | Design for Degradation Design Benign Chemicals | Pollution prevention Design for Energy Efficiency | Accident Prevention EV0000632 · EV0000633 · EV0000634

Source slide page 6 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Prevent Waste : Synthesis only targeted product with minimum or no by products/waste If waste is produced , it involves cost and time to treat and safely dispose it, as a result production cost increases EV0000635 · EV0000636 · EV0000637
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Atom Economy : Synthetic methods should be designed to maximize the incorporation of all materials used into the final product The chemical processes should have maximum product yield Atom economy can be calculated using the relation % Atom Economy = (FW of atoms utilized/FW of all reactants) X 100 Atom Economy in a Substitution Reaction - moderate Atom Economy in Elimination Reactions - low Atom Economy in Addition Reactions -100% Atom Economy in Rearrangement Reactions -100% For a green synthesis atom economy must be maximum (100%) EV0000638 · EV0000639 · EV0000640
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Safer Synthesis : The synthetic processes should be designed so as to minimize or prevent the production of hazardous substances Necessary safety precautions have to be taken while processing to protect human health and environment e.g., Polyurethane is manufactured conventionally using isocyanate and alcohol. However, isocyanate is produced using phosgene, a toxic gas  The alternative method by green synthesis of polyurethane eliminates the use of phosgene and uses CO2 EV0000641 · EV0000642 · EV0000643
  9. Source slide page 9 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Safe Products : Chemical products should be designed to preserve efficacy of function while reducing toxicity Products should not have any side effects on the people using it and on the environment It is envisaged to produce the chemical products specially those being used in cosmetics, pharmaceuticals, etc. EV0000644 · EV0000645 · EV0000646
  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Design Benign Chemicals : Use safer solvents and reaction conditions Avoid using solvents, separation agents, or other auxiliary chemicals If these chemicals are necessary, use innocuous chemicals Chemical syntheses are assisted by solvent medium: recommended to use alternative solvents , greener in nature such as aqueous medium, liquid carbon-dioxide, ionic liquids(N-alkyl pyridinium cation with BF4- ) , or solvent free systems Avoid harmful solvents like chloroform, pyridine which are known to cause health hazard, are carcinogenic to human beings and animals or cause severe damage to environment EV0000647 · EV0000648 · EV0000649
  11. Source slide page 11 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Design for Energy efficiency : Chemical processes are to be designed in such a way that they are less energy intensive reactions occurring at mild conditions require less time to complete For this purpose it is worthwhile to utilize Bio-catalyst, homogeneous and heterogeneous catalyst which reduces the energy of activation Modern techniques such as solvent free synthesis, supercritical fluid systems, microwave irradiation, and ultrasound EV0000650 · EV0000651 · EV0000652
  12. Source slide page 12 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Use of renewable feedstock : Raw material or feedstock used as starting material should be renewable rather than depleting wherever technically and economically possible Use of renewable feedstock such as biomass and agricultural wastes are strongly recommended rather than using non-renewable resources such as coal, petroleum-based raw materials, etc. EV0000653 · EV0000654 · EV0000655
  13. Source slide page 13 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Reduce Derivatives : Avoid unnecessary derivatization temporary modification should be avoided whenever possible as they generate waste the atom efficiency is reduced as the protective group is not incorporated in the final product e.g. blocking group, protection/ deprotection EV0000656 · EV0000657 · EV0000658
  14. Source slide page 14 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Catalysis : Use catalytic reactions, as catalysts Speed up the reaction can be recycled and are highly selective less energy consumption during reaction obtaining high yield of product of high purity Unlike stiochiometric reactions which are energy intensive require excessive reagents EV0000659 · EV0000660 · EV0000661
  15. Source slide page 15 (extracted text): ENGINEERING CHEMISTRY Green chemistry Design for Degradation : Design chemicals and products to degrade after use to innocuous substances after use so that they do not accumulate in the environment Especially applicable to insecticides pesticides polymers which tend to persist in the environment and are known to cause bioaccumulations EV0000662 · EV0000663 · EV0000664

Overview of Hydrogen Storage Methods Form Advantages Disadvantages O Gaseous Form - Less infrastructure required - Needs extra energy for compression - More cost-effective - Occupies a large space - Can be compressed to high - Requires leak-proof tanks pressure Liquid Form - Exists at a very low temperature - Reinforced and insulated storage tank required (20K / -253°C) - Energy-intensive cooling and compression (~30% loss) - Stored in cryogenic tanks - Low volume Solid Form - Hydrogen can be released on - Currently under intensive Research & Development demand (e.g., by heat) (R&D) - Ease of handling - Enhanced safety s s s Key challenges of the materials and their solutions 1. Weak Binding Forces: Hydrogen storage materials have high specific surface areas but weak binding forces (e.g., Van der Waals forces). 2. Metal Decorations: Metal decorations are effective in increasing the bonding energy of hydrogen on sorption-based materials. 3. Hydrogen Spillover: e Hydrogen storage in carbon materials is enhanced by decorating these materials with transition metals. ¢ Definition: Hydrogen spillover refers to the migration of hydrogen atoms from the metal surface to anon-metal support or absorbent.
